| [2D Illustrated Art Projected Onto Simple 3D Primitives (Boxes, Cylinders)](entries/billboard-2d-art-on-3d-primitives.md) | agent-pattern | Game-dev environment-art technique for cutting poly count and art workload: instead of modeling detailed 3D geometry for environment pieces, an artist illustrates the assets as flat 2D art, which is then projected/mapped onto simple 3D primitive stand-in shapes (boxes, cylinders) that carry the actual scene geometry. The 2D art carries all visual detail and stylization while the underlying mesh stays trivially simple, letting a small team build varied, stylized environments quickly and keep scene poly count low for performance across a wide hardware range (including Steam Deck). Demonstrated by the two-person dev team of the upcoming Unreal Engine roguelike 'Barrow and Blade' as part of an optimization pass alongside occlusion culling, baked lighting, and object pooling. | game-design, environment-art, texture-projection, stylized-rendering, poly-count-optimization, game-development, unreal-engine, steam-deck |

| [Agent Dispatcher — repository retrieval and specialist-role routing for Claude Code and Codex](entries/agent-dispatcher.md) | framework | Claude Code plugin and Codex skill pack that routes a task to one of 27 specialist roles (engineering, design, testing, research, writing, operations) and assembles a bounded context packet of source excerpts using symbols, source text, code relationships, Git history, and stack-trace frames — rather than a full-repo dump. Ships 79 bundled local skills, references 31 external skills, 8 workflow recipes, a registry of 19 MCP servers, and 50 detection signals for role/skill selection. Standard retrieval runs locally with no model or network calls; optional features (model-assisted reranking, an external 'Jev' decision-engine API, an Onboarding Explorer, procedural learning from recorded outcomes) are off by default and require the operator's own provider configuration. Installs via `claude plugin marketplace add` + `claude plugin install` for Claude Code, or `python3 install_codex.py` for Codex (installs one skill to `~/.agents/skills/agent-dispatcher/`). Invoked with `/agent-dispatcher:agent-dispatcher <request>` (Claude) or `$agent-dispatcher <request>` (Codex); roles can be forced directly (e.g. `$agent-dispatcher reviewer`). Verification is explicit: each role defines what evidence counts as done and reports which checks ran, passed, or were left unverified. Requires Python 3.10+, Git, and an authenticated Claude Code or Codex; native Windows is unsupported (WSL recommended). MIT license, single maintainer, repo created 2026-09-19 with ~50 GitHub stars and 4 forks as of this review. | claude-code-plugin, codex-skill, repository-retrieval, role-routing, context-engineering, multi-agent, mcp, verification, code-search, git-history |

| [Agent-Written Procedural Generation Over Generative-AI Assets (Game Dev)](entries/procedural-code-over-generative-ai-gamedev.md) | agent-pattern | Game-dev technique/philosophy for AI-accelerated development — rather than integrating generative mesh-generation tools (e.g. Meshy), 3D-editing-software MCP integrations (e.g. a Blender MCP server), prebuilt game engines, or narrow pre-written markdown "skills," have a coding agent write custom procedural-generation code directly, encoding visual style and geometry in math/code so the same deterministic logic regenerates consistent results across every object instead of re-running a generative model per asset; animation is produced the same way, via deterministic algorithms rather than pre-made or model-generated clips; reported tradeoff is that procedural logic can fail in ways generative output does not (a demonstrated prototype had a spider whose procedurally generated legs ended up inverted), and the achievable art style is constrained relative to what a generative model could produce. | procedural-generation, game-development, code-generation, deterministic-animation, anti-slop, claude-code, vibe-coding |

| [AgentChassis — contract-first orchestration for long-running coding agents](entries/agentchassis.md) | framework | Contract-first orchestration that splits agentic coding into non-overlapping orchestrator/worker/reviewer roles under one rule: the agent that defines the work cannot implement it. Every task is a durable work record stating scope, acceptance criteria, and validation before any code is written; a task missing those is rejected before any model runs (deterministic check, no model call). Workers are confined to their declared file scope (Linux bwrap sandbox where available); reviewers check each change against its contract; every run records honest enforcement provenance (enforced true/false plus isolation backend). Parallel agents run on non-overlapping scopes without collision, interrupted tasks resume from their record, and the repo accumulates an engineering record of what was planned, why, and whether it passed. Drives multiple agent CLIs (Codex, Claude, and more) through a typed MCP interface. Source-available under Elastic License 2.0 as public @agent-chassis/* npm packages; an optional hosted Chassis Control Engine (CCE, private beta) adds org-level admission and signed attestation. | multi-agent, orchestration, code-agent, work-records, sandbox, mcp, git-native, provenance, runtime-governance, elv2 |

| [Blockout Technique for Pixel Art Animation (Color-Coded Body-Part Blobs)](entries/blockout-pixel-art-animation.md) | agent-pattern | Game-dev animation technique for pixel art sprites. Animating detailed pixel art frame by frame from the start is slow — each pose requires precise pixel placement, and fixing a bad pose means redoing detail work. A blockout instead assigns each body part (arm, leg, head, torso, weapon) a distinct flat color with no internal detail, producing a rough colored-blob version of the sprite. The full animation is blocked out first using only these color blobs, which is faster to pose and easier to revise since there is no detail to preserve; the blockout can be previewed and tested in-engine to validate timing and motion before any detail work begins. Once the blocked-out motion reads correctly, each pose is detailed individually — adding detail on top of an established pose is reported as easier than freehanding a detailed pose from scratch. Framed as an extension of the general game-dev 'blockout' practice (used for roughing in level geometry/environment art before detailing) applied to sprite animation, and overlaps with the traditional animation concepts of blocking/pose-to-pose workflows and silhouette-based pose readability checks. | game-design, pixel-art, animation, blockout, blocking, sprite-animation, game-development, godot |

| [Chip-Damage Health Bars and Color-Coded Floating Damage Numbers (Hit-Feedback Game-Feel Techniques)](entries/health-bar-chip-damage-feedback.md) | agent-pattern | Game-dev UI/feedback techniques for making hit-point damage feel satisfying without changing the underlying numbers. Health bar — on damage, a pale/orange "chip" trail is left behind at the bar's prior fill level and holds briefly before draining down to the new value (rather than snapping instantly), so the player sees how much was lost; the bar also shakes, flashes, and punches on each hit; its color shifts green→yellow→red as a threshold-based danger signal; healing fills back in smoothly with a sparkle effect at the fill edge. Floating damage numbers — numbers pop in, float upward, and fade out over roughly half a second to just over a second; rapid consecutive hits stack and arc to alternating sides so overlapping numbers stay legible; number size scales with hit magnitude; numbers are color-coded by type (e.g. white/normal, orange/critical, green/heal); critical hits render bold and with extra shake. Presented as a reusable feedback layer independent of the underlying damage math — the same damage value reads as more or less impactful purely from how it is animated and rendered. | game-design, game-feel, health-bar, damage-numbers, floating-combat-text, ui-feedback, juice, chip-damage, game-development |

| [Cutting Anticipation Frames for Input Responsiveness (Diablo 3 Barbarian Swing, Mario Jump)](entries/cut-anticipation-frames-responsiveness.md) | agent-pattern | Game-dev technique for making player-controlled actions feel responsive by removing, rather than speeding up, the anticipation phase of an animation. Traditional animation splits an action into three roughly equal parts — anticipation (wind-up), action, and follow-through — but the anticipation portion delays the visible response to player input. Cites classic platformers (e.g. Super Mario), where the character begins moving upward immediately on jump input instead of crouching first, and Diablo 3's early development, where a barbarian's two-handed weapon swing originally reached back from a neutral pose before striking; speeding up that wind-up still read as sluggish and lost its sense of weight, while cutting the roughly 5–6 frames (about 180ms) of anticipation entirely — so the character instantly snaps to the fully wound-up pose the instant the player clicks — fixed the responsiveness complaints. Frames the fix in terms of silhouette — an instant, dramatic change in the character's on-screen shape gives the player an immediate visual read that the input registered, substituting for the anticipation frames as the feedback signal. | game-design, game-feel, juice, animation-principles, anticipation, input-responsiveness, snappy-controls, frame-data, silhouette, game-development |

| [Directing and Verifying AI Coding Agents (What Separates Strong Engineers)](entries/directing-and-verifying-coding-agents.md) | agent-pattern | Findings from a founder's informal benchmark scoring ~60 engineers (0-1000) on how well they direct, review, and verify a coding agent on a real task with deliberately planted half-failures: the dominant skill gap was specification quality and review behavior, not prompting; top scorers verify mechanism (which files changed, whether test files changed) rather than output alone, since output-only review never catches an agent weakening a test to go green; checking between turns beats one review at the end; a task costing 3x its neighbors signals a badly cut task, not inherent difficulty; and detecting a half-failure without an owner to act on it is worthless. | agent-pattern, code-review, verification, ai-coding-agents, spec-quality, benchmark, human-skill |
| [Disney's Animation Principles Applied to Game VFX (Anticipation, Squash & Stretch, Follow Through, Timing, Arcs)](entries/disney-animation-principles-game-vfx.md) | agent-pattern | Game-dev technique for making a combat VFX effect (demonstrated on a thrown fireball) read as satisfying by applying five of Disney's twelve classical animation principles instead of adding more particles or better shaders — anticipation (the hero leans back while the fire gathers, then throws), squash and stretch (the fireball stretches in flight, the enemy squashes on hit), follow through (the hero's body settles after the throw, the trail lags behind, the burst flies on), timing (freeze the game for a few frames on the hit, i.e. hit-stop), and arcs (the fireball, sparks, and damage numbers travel on curved rather than straight paths). Framed as the same underlying asset read as flat or alive purely based on which of these principles are layered on top, independent of particle count or shader quality. | game-design, game-feel, juice, animation-principles, squash-and-stretch, anticipation, follow-through, hit-stop, vfx, game-development |

| [Five-Beat Boss Entrance Sequence (Lock, Wait, Reveal, Name, Sell-the-Size)](entries/boss-fight-entrance-design.md) | agent-pattern | Game-dev technique for staging a boss encounter's opening moments as a five-beat sequence rather than a plain spawn-and-health-bar pop-in — lock the arena (seal the path the player entered by, e.g. collapsing it, removing retreat as an option), make the player wait (empty arena, low rumble, dust settling, building tension before the boss appears), reveal the boss slowly by panning the camera from its feet up to its face rather than showing it all at once, slam its name on screen in large text, fill a large health bar from empty rather than showing it already full (communicating scale), then sell its size with a low camera angle, screen-shaking footsteps, and a roar that triggers screen shake, a dust blast, and arena-wide reaction. Framed as reusable staging independent of the specific boss's stats or moveset — the same boss model reads as a non-event or as a threat purely based on how the entrance is directed. | game-design, boss-fight, boss-design, game-feel, cutscene-design, camera-work, screen-shake, health-bar, game-development |
| [Five-Effect Card Feel Technique (Hover, Pick Up, Sway, Snap, Sound)](entries/card-feel-five-effects.md) | agent-pattern | Game-dev technique for making a playable card feel satisfying instead of flat, via five layered effects applied on top of an otherwise unchanged card-in-hand interaction — (1) hover, where sliding across the hand pops each card up and leans it toward the cursor, though grabbing one barely lifts it off the table; (2) pick up, where lifting a card high spreads its shadow outward beneath it, though the card stays visually flat while dragged; (3) sway, where the card leans in the direction it's dragged and swings back when released, though it originally teleported into place on drop; (4) snap, where the card flies into its target slot, overshoots, and settles, with the rest of the hand sliding over to close the gap, though this step is silent; (5) sound, adding a soft flick noise on grab and a slap noise on landing. Framed as a reusable five-layer pipeline — the same baseline card-in-hand interaction (described as "about as fun as filling in a form") reads as a satisfying "big play" purely from how many of these layers are stacked on top of it, independent of the card game's actual rules or content. | game-design, card-game, game-feel, juice, drag-and-drop, ui-feedback, sound-design, animation, game-development |
| [Five-Step Dialogue Presentation Technique (Typewriter, Pauses, Sound, Text Effects, Speaker Animation)](entries/dialogue-feel-five-steps.md) | agent-pattern | Game-dev technique for making NPC dialogue feel alive instead of appearing as a flat wall of text, via five layered presentation steps — (1) typewriter effect reveals letters one at a time so the player reads along at the character's pace; (2) pauses hold a short beat on each comma and period, giving the line timing instead of a flat reading speed; (3) a tiny sound blip plays on every revealed letter, pitched to suit the speaking character; (4) text effects apply per-word motion keyed to tone, e.g. happy words wave and angry words shake; (5) the speaker's portrait or model bobs while talking and its facial expression switches between states (e.g. happy to furious) tied to the line's content. Framed as a reusable layered presentation pipeline independent of the actual dialogue content — the same line of text reads as flat or as a performed character purely from how these five steps are applied on top of it. | game-design, dialogue-system, game-feel, typewriter-effect, text-effects, sound-design, ui-feedback, game-development |
| [Flattened Secondary Mesh as a Stylized Fake Shadow (Instead of Dynamic Shadow Rendering)](entries/flattened-mesh-fake-shadows.md) | agent-pattern | Game-dev shadow technique that replaces an engine's built-in dynamic shadow system with a hand-controlled stand-in: a second, simplified version of the character mesh is flattened onto the floor via a material, and that flattened silhouette is used as the character's shadow. Reported benefits are twofold — it saves the performance cost of real-time shadow/lighting calculations, and it gives the artist direct control over the shadow's shape and softness so it can be made 'punchy' and stylized to match the game's art style rather than looking like a physically-accurate soft/dynamic shadow. Demonstrated in Unreal Engine by the two-person dev team of the upcoming roguelike 'Barrow and Blade' as one of three non-standard optimization techniques. | game-design, stylized-rendering, shadows, performance-optimization, material-technique, game-development, unreal-engine |

| [Functional Differentiation of Status Effects (Fire/Poison/Bleed by Role, Not Magnitude)](entries/status-effect-functional-differentiation.md) | agent-pattern | Game-dev design technique for avoiding redundant damage-over-time status effects — when fire, poison, and bleed differ only in how much damage they deal, players converge on the single strongest one and the rest go unused; the fix is giving each effect a distinct mechanical role instead of a different number. | [keepwarm (Claude Code prompt-cache keep-warm mod)](entries/keepwarm.md) | plugin | Claude Code mod (early-access plugin/hooks feature) that keeps an idle interactive session's prompt cache warm by forking a tool-less, text-free copy of the session's own transcript as a cache-read ping once 57 minutes pass with no model request — restarts the 1-hour cache TTL without adding anything to the conversation; stops after 20h since the last real prompt, after a refresh that reads under 1,000 cached tokens, or if a refresh gets no reply; turns itself off for a session if a refresh has to write more of the conversation than it reads (cache unreachable — 6 of 132 refreshes on record did this); never runs under claude -p or the SDK. Controlled via /keepwarm on|off|status and env vars (KEEPWARM_OFF, KEEPWARM_IDLE_MIN, KEEPWARM_TTL_MIN, KEEPWARM_MAX_HOURS, KEEPWARM_PROMPT); state kept locally in ~/.claude/state/keepwarm/<session id>.json. | [Knowledge in the Folder, Behavior in the Model: A Fine-Tuning vs. RAG Split](entries/knowledge-folder-behavior-model-split.md) | agent-pattern | A three-step strategy for deciding what to fine-tune versus what to retrieve: keep facts (pricing, policies, specs) in documents the model reads at answer time rather than baking them into weights, since fine-tuning changes model behavior, not factual recall, so tuning on company documents still produces factual errors; tune only for behavior (output format, tone, how to answer a complaint) using a few hundred clean, human-approved examples of one task rather than thousands of unreviewed ones; and do that tuning on a small open-weight model run on a single workstation, A/B tested against the untuned baseline before deployment. | fine-tuning, rag, knowledge-vs-behavior, open-weight-models, sft, evaluation, model-ownership |

| [Nine Slicing (9-Slice) for Scalable UI Panels](entries/nine-slicing-ui-panels.md) | agent-pattern | Game-dev UI technique for resizing a panel/box sprite to any dimension without distorting its border art. Naively scaling a textured panel along an axis stretches the corners and looks worse the more it's scaled. Nine slicing splits the source image into a 3x3 grid — four corners, four edges, one center — and scales each region differently when the panel resizes: corners are repositioned but never scaled (so border art stays crisp), each edge stretches along only its own axis to connect the corners, and the center stretches on both axes to fill the middle. Requires only that the edge/center art be drawn so it tiles or stretches cleanly; built into most major engines as a first-class primitive (Godot's NinePatchRect/StyleBoxTexture, Unity's Sliced Sprite image type, and CSS's border-image-slice), so adopting it needs no custom tooling. | game-design, nine-slice, nine-patch, ui, sprite-scaling, game-development, godot, unity |
| [Nine-Step Juicy Damage Number Technique (Pop, Float, Fade, Arc, Scale, Color, Crit FX, Stack, Randomize)](entries/juicy-damage-numbers.md) | agent-pattern | Game-dev technique for upgrading a default floating-damage-number label into "juicy" feedback through a stack of independent, optional tweaks rather than one fix — pick a legible display font (the reel uses Bungee from Google Fonts), pop the number in on spawn instead of just appearing, float it upward as it arrives, fade it out at the end of its lifetime, give each digit an arc-like falling offset instead of a straight rise, scale its size by the damage value so bigger hits read as bigger, shift its color over time based on which action produced it, layer particles and screen shake onto critical hits using the same logic, experimentally stack numbers on very fast multi-hit attacks, and randomize each number's spawn offset so a burst of hits doesn't render as a single overlapping stack. Framed as a menu of optional, independently-applicable steps rather than a required pipeline, and as engine-agnostic (the creator states it applies to any game engine). | game-design, damage-numbers, game-feel, juice, ui-feedback, vfx, screen-shake, particles, game-development |
| [Noise-Panning Foliage Animation (Perlin Noise Vertex Displacement)](entries/noise-panning-foliage-animation.md) | agent-pattern | Game-dev technique for animating large numbers of static foliage/vegetation meshes cheaply by driving per-object or per-vertex movement from a Perlin noise texture instead of hand-animating each piece — pixel brightness sets how much an object moves, continuously panning the noise across the environment avoids visible looping, and layering several noise patterns at different speeds adds variation; turns one static art asset into apparently-living foliage across hundreds of instances without per-object animation clips. | procedural-animation, perlin-noise, shader-technique, foliage, vegetation, wind-animation, vertex-displacement, game-development, unity |

| [Persistence Keys for Entity State Across Area Unload/Reload (Seamless Streaming Worlds)](entries/persistence-key-entity-state.md) | agent-pattern | Game-dev technique for keeping entity state (e.g. a defeated enemy) consistent when a seamless streaming world unloads and reloads the area containing it, without a full save/load pass. Problem: in games with invisible/seamless loading zones (as opposed to distinct rooms, where re-spawning enemies on re-entry reads as normal), an unloaded enemy's defeat is lost — walking back into a previously-cleared area respawns the enemy, breaking immersion. Fix: a small piece of state (a 'persistence key' / flag), identified by the entity's own name/ID, is written to an object that never unloads (e.g. the player character) at the moment the entity is defeated; when the area streams back in and the entity's scene re-instantiates, it checks the persistent object for its own key before running its normal spawn logic, loading in a defeated state if the key is present. Demonstrated in an indie 2D pixel-art game (enemy 'Runk Tonk', persistent holder 'Isadora') without naming a specific engine's API, though captioned with Godot tags. The same pattern generalizes to any stable-ID-keyed state delta kept outside the unloadable scene graph — analogous to dedicated streaming-persistence systems in other engines (e.g. Unreal's SPUD plugin and native LevelStreamingPersistenceManager) and to the broader save-system architecture principle of writing deltas keyed by stable IDs to a store decoupled from runtime objects, rather than serializing live scene state. | game-design, state-persistence, entity-state, streaming-world, loading-zones, respawn-prevention, level-design, game-development, godot |

| [Production RAG as a System (8-Part Taxonomy)](entries/production-rag-system-taxonomy.md) | agent-pattern | Practitioner taxonomy framing production RAG as an 8-part system (not a vector-database feature): 01 offline build time (parse/chunk/embed/index), 02 online query time (rewrite/retrieve/rerank/assemble/generate), 03 knowledge store (vectors+metadata+raw docs+freshness), 04 retrieval quality (hybrid search/filters/top-k/reranking), 05 answer quality (grounding/citations/structured output/fallback), 06 state/data layer (Postgres+Redis+object storage+vector index), 07 observability/evals (latency/recall/answer quality/hallucinations/cost), 08 safety operations (ACLs/PII protection/prompt-injection defense/caching/feedback); presented as a directory-tree mental model contrasting with the four-step chunk-embed-retrieve-prompt demo pipeline. | rag, production-rag, retrieval, taxonomy, system-design, observability, safety, agent-pattern |

| [Pseudo-3D via Sprite Billboarding Over 2D Gameplay Logic, with Fog-Based Distance Culling](entries/pseudo-3d-sprite-billboarding.md) | agent-pattern | Game-dev rendering technique for making a fully 2D top-down game look 2.5D/3D without changing its gameplay logic. The camera is tilted down from a pure overhead view, and top-down sprites are 'stood up' and rotated to face the camera via a single matrix transform in code (classic sprite billboarding, as in Pokemon Black & White-style pseudo-3D). Hit detection stays simple circle hitboxes and elevation/jump checks stay a plain numeric comparison between grid cells, both unaware of the new camera angle. Tilting the camera low exposed a far draw distance that tanked frame rate; hiding distant geometry in fog and simplifying far props (trees) recovered performance, reported as a 7x speedup. Demonstrated in a devlog for the in-development game 'Soul Stream.' | game-design, billboarding, pseudo-3d, sprite-rendering, top-down, matrix-transform, collision-detection, distance-culling, fog-rendering, game-development, devlog |

Repo created 2026-04-14, single primary maintainer (1,566 commits as of review), MIT license for REA-authored code (third-party dependencies and commercial decompilers like IDA/Hopper/JEB keep their own licenses and require the user's own authorization). | [Reusable Node Components for Shared Enemy/Entity Behavior (Godot Composition Over Inheritance)](entries/godot-node-composition-components.md) | agent-pattern | Game-dev architecture technique for sharing behavior across many entity types without duplicating code or using deep inheritance chains: break each behavior into a small, self-contained node (e.g. a health component that stores health, subtracts damage, and fires a signal when it reaches zero; a hit-detection component; a damage-on-touch component; a death-drop component), each of which knows nothing about the specific enemy it's attached to. A new enemy is assembled by dropping in the needed components, wiring their signals, and tuning numbers — only animation work is enemy-specific. Improving a shared component improves every entity using it at once. This is Godot's established community pattern of composition over inheritance via nodes and signals, not a technique unique to one project; demonstrated in a dev-log reel where 76 enemies in the roguelike Capy of Gods (built in Godot) run on the same shared health component. | game-design, composition-over-inheritance, component-pattern, godot, signals, architecture, game-development, roguelike, code-reuse |

| [Rogue's 3x3 Grid ("Tic-Tac-Toe") Dungeon Generation Algorithm](entries/rogue-1980-tic-tac-toe-dungeon-generation.md) | agent-pattern | Procedural dungeon-layout algorithm from Rogue (1980, by Michael Toy and Glenn Wichman, later joined by Ken Arnold), the game that gave the roguelike genre its name. Rather than placing rooms at arbitrary positions — which collide and can leave rooms unreachable — Rogue's source (version 5.4, files rooms.c and passages.c) divides its 80x24 terminal screen into a fixed 3x3 grid of nine cells. Each cell gets at most one room, randomly sized and positioned but confined to its cell so rooms can never overlap or share a wall; up to three cells (equally likely to be 0-3) are left empty, acting only as a point corridors pass through. Cells are then linked one at a time, each new link joining an already-connected cell to an unconnected neighbor, until all nine are reachable from the starting room, plus up to four extra random links so the layout isn't a single unique path. The result is a layout that is random but always fully connected and always resolvable within a single draw call's worth of simple grid math. | procedural-generation, dungeon-generation, roguelike, grid-based-generation, level-design, game-development, history |

| [Start Cheap, Escalate on Fail: Measured Model/Effort Routing](entries/start-cheap-escalate-on-fail-routing.md) | agent-pattern | A measured cost-routing strategy for coding agents: define a task as work with a pass/fail test (the SWE-bench model — FAIL_TO_PASS plus PASS_TO_PASS), always attempt it first on the cheapest model/effort tier, run the test, and only escalate model/effort on failure. Demonstrated on 6 SWE-bench tasks (2 quick/medium/hard) across Sonnet, Opus, and Fable at varying effort: on one quick task, Fable at max effort cost $2.10/~5min while Sonnet at low effort cost $0.07/14s and both passed the identical test. Even counting the cost of failed escalation attempts, starting cheap came out cheaper across all six tasks tested. Suggests logging (task features, tier tried, pass/fail, cost) and fitting a classifier (e.g. scikit-learn) on that log to build a task router that predicts where to start for a given task shape. | model-routing, cost-optimization, swe-bench, agent-pattern, effort-tiering, task-classification, scikit-learn |

| [Storing Per-Pixel Time and Location in a Texture to Drive Ground Effects (Instead of Spawned Decals/Actors)](entries/texture-time-ground-effects.md) | agent-pattern | Game-dev technique for rendering ground effects (e.g. area-of-effect visuals) without spawning a decal or actor object per effect instance. Time-of-occurrence and location data is written into a texture; a material reads that texture to control how the effect visually appears and disappears over time. A separate data-driven grid system tracks which grid cells a character currently occupies, used to decide when to trigger or clear an effect, rather than each effect instance independently tracking its own lifetime and overlap with other effects. Reported benefit is avoiding the lifetime/overlap bookkeeping cost that scales with the number of independently-spawned decal/actor effects. Demonstrated in Unreal Engine by the two-person dev team of the upcoming roguelike 'Barrow and Blade' as one of three non-standard optimization techniques. | game-design, shader-technique, render-target, ground-effects, performance-optimization, data-driven-grid, game-development, unreal-engine |

| [SWE-bench](entries/swe-bench.md) | reference | Coding-agent benchmark built from real GitHub issues across (originally) 12 popular Python repositories: an agent generates a patch from the issue description and codebase, graded by human-developer-written unit tests split into FAIL_TO_PASS (the issue's own test must flip from failing to passing) and PASS_TO_PASS (all pre-existing tests must stay green). Variants: Full (complete dataset), Lite (lower-cost subset), Verified (500 human-annotated, solvable tasks, OpenAI-developed 2024), and Pro (Scale AI SEAL lab — harder, multi-file, cross-repository-structure tasks under a standardized 250-turn scaffold). By October 2026, SWE-bench Verified is widely considered no longer a reliable yardstick for frontier models (leaderboard stagnant since December 2025, contamination concerns), and even SWE-bench Pro had documented reward-hacking and task-quality issues (102 flagged instances), corrected in a 'Pro Verified' release. Earlier research (SWE-Bench+) found solution leakage in 18 Lite and 37 Verified instances. Derivative benchmarks address staleness: SWE-bench-Live (rolling weekly fresh issues) and SWEBench-verified-mini (50-issue reduced set). | benchmark, coding-agents, github-issues, swe-bench, evaluation, contamination, reward-hacking |

| [The 7 Levels of Observability](entries/seven-levels-of-observability.md) | agent-pattern | A content creator's seven-stage observability maturity ladder — logs, metrics, traces, correlation (shared request ID across logs/traces), alerts, SLOs (SLI/SLO/SLA), and cardinality management — presented as a progression where each stage answers a question the previous one couldn't (what happened, is it trending, where is the time going, why, before users notice, is the target at risk, can we afford to measure it). Not a single named industry-standard framework: published observability maturity models elsewhere range from 3 pillars (logs/metrics/traces) to 4-5 level maturity scales (e.g. Last9, codefarm.in) covering the same underlying concepts — structured logs with trace IDs, OpenTelemetry-style distributed tracing, SLO/error-budget-driven alerting, and cardinality limits on metric labels — under different level counts and names. | observability, logs, metrics, traces, opentelemetry, correlation, alerting, slo, sli, sla, cardinality, devops, sre, monitoring |

| [Tileless Terrain Rendering via Per-Pixel Four-Corner Blending Shader](entries/tileless-terrain-corner-blend-shader.md) | agent-pattern | Game-dev terrain-rendering technique that replaces hand-drawn tileset art entirely with a single shader. Each map cell stores one terrain-type number instead of a chosen tile sprite; a shader reads the four cell values surrounding each screen pixel and renders a terrain layer at that pixel only when it covers more than half of those four corners, producing automatically-rounded coastlines, grass/dirt edges, and other transitions with no pre-drawn edge, corner, or inner-corner art. | [Token-Budget Workflow for Small-Context Local Coding Agents](entries/token-budget-local-agent-workflow.md) | agent-pattern | Practitioner workflow for making a heavily quantized local LLM (32K-65K context) usable as a coding agent despite its narrow context window and higher error rate: (1) pick a harness with a small startup token footprint (author reports Pi needs ~1.5K tokens vs. an estimated 10-14K for Codex/Claude Code, leaving more of a 64K window for actual work) instead of a heavier, feature-rich harness; (2) split every task into a planning turn that writes plan.md, then clear the session (Pi's /new) and run a separate implementation turn that reads plan.md and records results in it, so neither turn needs the whole task history in context; (3) for plans too large for one implementation turn, delegate via a subtask-runner skill (subtask4llm) that executes up to 10 subtasks sequentially in fresh, isolated agent contexts, feeding results back to the coordinating instance; (4) compensate for the model's lower quality at low-bit quantization by enforcing hard constraints in AGENTS.md — a 300-500 line file-size ceiling, one responsibility per module, mandatory unit tests for every function/change, tests-and-build must pass before done, no commits without explicit user request — which the author attributes to a remark by Robert Martin (Uncle Bob, author of Clean Code) that he has stopped reading AI-generated code, substituting enforced constraints for manual review and spending free local-GPU iterations letting the model self-correct against those constraints. | agent-pattern, local-llm, context-management, plan-md, agents-md, token-budget, task-decomposition, pi-coding-agent |

| [Vector Database Cost Economics](entries/vector-database-cost-economics.md) | reference | Practitioner cost model for vector databases: storage bytes = vector count × dimensions × precision (1M 768-dim float32 vectors ≈ 3GB before index/metadata overhead; dimensions scale storage linearly, so 3072-dim embeddings cost 4x what 768-dim cost); RAM-resident indexes cost roughly 10-50x more per GB than SSD, which is roughly 10x object storage (~$0.023/GB-month raw S3-class rate); production replication (2-3x) and HNSW-style index overhead (1.5-2x raw vector bytes) compound on top; query/write pricing (per-request, per-unit, or folded into storage) can dominate the bill at high QPS independent of storage cost. | vector-database, cost-modeling, pricing, storage-economics, object-storage, quantization, rag |

| [VS Code Port Forwarding via Dev Tunnels (Public Localhost Sharing)](entries/vscode-port-forwarding-devtunnels.md) | agent-pattern | Technique for sharing a locally-running dev server with anyone over the internet using VS Code's built-in Ports panel — run the project, note the port, open the Ports view, use Forward a Port, then open the generated URL's context menu and set Port Visibility to Public so viewers reach it without GitHub sign-in. The underlying mechanism is Microsoft Dev Tunnels (URLs resolve to *.devtunnels.ms); forwarded ports default to Private (requires sign-in with the same GitHub account used to start forwarding) and must be explicitly switched to Public to work as a no-login share link. Works for any local server (the reel demonstrates a full-stack Vite/React PDF-tools clone) as long as the process keeps running on the local machine — the tunnel relays traffic, it does not host the app independently. | vscode, port-forwarding, dev-tunnels, localhost-sharing, web-dev, networking, remote-access, demo-sharing |
| [WaveFunctionCollapse](entries/wave-function-collapse.md) | framework | C# algorithm (by Maxim Gumin) that generates bitmaps/tilemaps locally similar to a single input example — picks the lowest-entropy region, collapses it to one of the input's NxN patterns weighted by their input frequency, and propagates that constraint outward, repeating until the whole output is determined or a contradiction forces a retry; used for procedural terrain, dungeon, and texture generation; 25.5k stars, 1.4k forks; MIT (sample images/tiles excluded from the license). | procedural-generation, wave-function-collapse, tile-generation, constraint-propagation, csharp, game-development, unity |
| [Weaviate](entries/weaviate.md) | framework | Go-based AI-native vector database — stores objects with vectors, integrated vectorization via OpenAI/Cohere/HuggingFace modules, semantic + BM25 keyword + hybrid search, built-in RAG and reranking, ACORN filtered search, multi-tenancy with RBAC, object TTL, gRPC/REST/GraphQL APIs; BSD-3-Clause | vector-database, semantic-search, hybrid-search, rag, go, embeddings, multi-tenancy, reranking |
| [Web Artifacts Builder](entries/web-artifacts-builder.md) | skill | use when markdown isn't enough for interactive reports/dashboards | html, reports, dashboards, visualization |
| [Webcam Feedback Loop for Agent Hardware/Firmware Debugging](entries/webcam-feedback-loop-hardware-debug.md) | agent-pattern | Anecdote of a coding agent (Opus 5.5 via Claude Code) suggesting its own instrumentation for a hardware task it couldn't otherwise observe: pointing a webcam at a physical ESP32-driven display so the agent could see the live rendered output while tuning Waveshare display-driver settings for portrait-mode frame rate, going from 4fps to 60fps in a few minutes once it had that visual feedback loop. | agent-pattern, hardware, embedded, esp32, visual-feedback, debugging, firmware, computer-vision |
| [webos-debloat (lucataco)](entries/lucataco-webos-debloat.md) | cli-tool | Single shell script for a rooted LG webOS TV that hides launcher bloat tiles and background apps, stops those apps from preloading into RAM, and disables home-screen promotions, sports alerts, recommendations and LG's Live Plus (ACR) tracking by editing LG's own app block list, preload-manager policy and settings keys (never the root filesystem), making every change reversible without blocking firmware updates; tested on one 2026 LG OLED (webOS 26, firmware 11.2). | webos, lg, debloat, shell-script, root, preload-manager, telemetry, acr, privacy |
| [webosbrew (Homebrew Channel for LG webOS)](entries/webosbrew.md) | framework | Community hub for rooting LG webOS Smart TVs — hosts the Homebrew Channel (a sideloaded on-device app store/manager unlocking apps like Kodi and Moonlight, ambient-light integrations such as HyperHDR, screensaver/wallpaper changes and remote-button remapping once installed), a directory of current software root exploits tracked per webOS version and patch date (faultmanager, DejaVuln, WTA, crashd, RootMy.TV, GetMeNow, plus a hardware DEBUG-flag method), and a model/firmware compatibility checker (CanI.RootMy.TV). | webos, lg, root, jailbreak, homebrew-channel, smart-tv, rooting, sideloading |
| [Weft](entries/weft-language.md) | framework | Novel AI orchestration language with typed nodes, durable execution, and human-in-the-loop primitives — early-stage but architecturally interesting | programming-language, ai-orchestration, durable-execution, visual-programming, human-in-the-loop, rust, orchestration |
| [WhisperX](entries/whisperx.md) | framework | Whisper + wav2vec2 forced alignment + pyannote speaker diarization — 70x realtime batched transcription with accurate word-level timestamps and speaker IDs; builds on faster-whisper; ideal for lecture/interview transcripts | asr, whisper, transcription, diarization, word-timestamps, speaker-id, audio |
| [Windows 11 God Mode](entries/windows-god-mode.md) | reference | Windows 10/11 trick — create a folder named GodMode.{ED7BA470-8E54-465E-825C-99712043E01C} to get a flat list of 200+ Control Panel shortcuts; useful for quick access to buried settings like environment variables, audio devices, power options | windows, tips, productivity |
| [Yarn Spinner](entries/yarn-spinner.md) | framework | Open-source dialogue system (Secret Lab) for writing and running branching game dialogue in a screenplay-like scripting language — the Yarn Spinner compiler/runtime is engine-agnostic, with an official Unity integration (YarnSpinner-Unity) sending lines, player-choice options, and in-scene commands from scripted conversations to the game; used in shipped titles including Night in the Woods, A Short Hike, and Dredge; Unity integration repo: 845 stars, 129 forks; MIT. | dialogue-system, narrative-scripting, unity, game-development, csharp |
| [You cannot automate a habit (discovery before automation)](entries/you-cannot-automate-habit.md) | agent-pattern | Practitioner reflection on building business automations/agents: the hard part is rarely the model or framework — it's that the target process only exists as an undocumented habit. Ask three people how it works and you get three answers (owner's original design, manager's exception-laden version, the operator's notebook-based reality); even 'the menu' has no single answer (per-outlet prices, time-of-day availability, duplicate names). The reusable practice: before automating, sit with people, force the business to write its own rules down for the first time, and get someone with authority to sign off on the version that will be encoded. | automation, agents, requirements, process-discovery, elicitation, consulting, workflow |

Docker setup and failure points), Nick Saraev (obscure automation triggers turned into business processes); requester also names Bilawal Sidhu, Greg Isenberg, Ras Mic as the desired non-salesy style | youtube, creators, agentic-workflows, n8n, automation, self-hosting, learning-resources, community-picks |
| [yt-dlp](entries/yt-dlp.md) | cli-tool | Feature-rich command-line audio/video downloader supporting thousands of sites — a community-maintained fork of youtube-dl with faster multi-threaded downloads, advanced format selection, subtitle embedding, and a plugin system for custom extractors/postprocessors; ~200k GitHub stars, releases roughly every 2 weeks; Unlicense (public domain); flagged by the music-industry group IFPI for EU piracy watchlist consideration, though IFPI itself notes its open-source/public-domain nature makes it hard to remove. | video-download, audio-download, cli, media-ingestion, youtube, ffmpeg, extractor-plugins, public-domain |
| [ZDR Router Trust — DeepSeek/Kimi/Xiaomi Data Leak Investigation (Sep 2026)](entries/zdr-router-trust.md) | reference | Chinese authorities investigating DeepSeek, Moonshot (Kimi), and Xiaomi for routing third-party router users' requests to Anthropic models, injecting hidden prompts to harvest CoT traces, modifying returned text, and training on the interactions — raising questions about ZDR (zero data retention) credibility of third-party model routers like OpenRouter and OpenCode | security, privacy, zdr, model-routing, openrouter, deepseek, kimi, anthropic, data-leak, trust |
| [Zed](entries/zed.md) | framework | Rust code editor (from the team behind Atom and Tree-sitter) with a native Agent Panel for agentic AI workflows — built-in agent reads/writes/runs code in a project; external coding agents (Claude, Codex, OpenCode, Copilot, Cursor, Gemini CLI, Qwen Code) connect via the Agent Client Protocol (ACP), with Zed hosting the thread UI while the external agent owns its runtime; inline edit predictions and multiplayer collaboration; editor core open-source, paid tiers sell Zed-hosted AI and admin controls; ~90k GitHub stars | code-editor, rust, agent-client-protocol, ai-agent, multiplayer, tree-sitter, acp |

## By Category

### agent-pattern

- [2D Illustrated Art Projected Onto Simple 3D Primitives (Boxes, Cylinders)](entries/billboard-2d-art-on-3d-primitives.md) — Game-dev environment-art technique for cutting poly count and art workload: instead of modeling detailed 3D geometry for environment pieces, an artist illustrates the assets as flat 2D art, which is then projected/mapped onto simple 3D primitive stand-in shapes (boxes, cylinders) that carry the actual scene geometry. The 2D art carries all visual detail and stylization while the underlying mesh stays trivially simple, letting a small team build varied, stylized environments quickly and keep scene poly count low for performance across a wide hardware range (including Steam Deck). Demonstrated by the two-person dev team of the upcoming Unreal Engine roguelike 'Barrow and Blade' as part of an optimization pass alongside occlusion culling, baked lighting, and object pooling.
- [3-Store Hybrid Memory Architecture for AI Agents](entries/three-store-hybrid-memory-architecture.md) — An architecture pattern arguing vector embeddings alone fail for AI agent memory, proposing three parallel stores — a vector store for fuzzy semantic matching, a key-value store for exact fact lookup, and a knowledge graph for multi-hop relationship reasoning — merged by a fusion layer
- [A Field Guide to Fable: Finding Your Unknowns](entries/field-guide-to-fable-finding-unknowns.md) — Anthropic engineer Thariq Shihipar's framework for working with capable AI models — the bottleneck shifts from model ability to specification quality; five techniques (blind-spot pass, interviews, references, rough mockups, brainstorms) for surfacing assumptions before they compound into costly rework; 2M views in 3 days
- [AAA Quality-Gate Multi-Agent Orchestration](entries/aaa-quality-gate-orchestration.md) — A prompt architecture for orchestrating multi-agent development with built-in quality verification — architecture-first design docs, screenshot-based verification loops, dependency-ordered builder waves, and a critic agent that scores against reference screenshots before passing quality gates
- [Advisor Strategy](entries/advisor-strategy.md) — architectural concept for cost-effective agent orchestration; executor/advisor split
- [Agent Cost Governors & Circuit Breakers](entries/agent-cost-governors.md) — Three-layer cost governance pattern for autonomous agents — hard token leases per step, circuit breakers on retry-without-progress, and step cost degradation (frontier → local model fallback) to prevent denial-of-wallet from recursive error loops
- [Agent-Driven Rooted LG webOS TV Debloat & Relaunch](entries/lg-webos-root-debloat-relaunch.md) — Prompt pattern for directing a coding agent (Claude Opus via Claude Code) over SSH/telnet to debloat and relaunch an already-rooted LG webOS TV: the owner roots the TV themselves first, then gives the agent an allowlist of apps/inputs to keep, named features to preserve, a permanent block on LG firmware updates and telemetry, a sub-second custom launcher with a fallback to stock Home, and safety rules (measure first, reversible/batched changes, never touch firmware partitions, write a handoff); cites a public debloat script as a named starting point to go beyond.
- [Agent-First Architecture (The LLM Calls the Code)](entries/agent-first-architecture.md) — Architecture pattern where a general coding agent (Claude Code, Codex, OpenCode) running on a VPS is the orchestrator, calling deterministic scripts and connectors from a repo, inverting the usual app-calls-LLM relationship
- [Agent-Written Procedural Generation Over Generative-AI Assets (Game Dev)](entries/procedural-code-over-generative-ai-gamedev.md) — Game-dev technique/philosophy for AI-accelerated development — rather than integrating generative mesh-generation tools (e.g. Meshy), 3D-editing-software MCP integrations (e.g. a Blender MCP server), prebuilt game engines, or narrow pre-written markdown "skills," have a coding agent write custom procedural-generation code directly, encoding visual style and geometry in math/code so the same deterministic logic regenerates consistent results across every object instead of re-running a generative model per asset; animation is produced the same way, via deterministic algorithms rather than pre-made or model-generated clips; reported tradeoff is that procedural logic can fail in ways generative output does not (a demonstrated prototype had a spider whose procedurally generated legs ended up inverted), and the achievable art style is constrained relative to what a generative model could produce.
- [Agentic RAG](entries/agentic-rag.md) — Agentic RAG wraps a vector-database retriever as a tool for an LLM agent, which reformulates queries, critiques retrieved snippets, and iterates through multiple retrieval rounds before synthesizing an answer
- [Agentic RAG with Terminal Tools (Direct Corpus Interaction)](entries/agentic-rag-terminal-tools.md) — Research pattern where LLM agents with terminal tool access (grep, find, head, cat) outperform embedding-based retrieval across 13+ benchmarks — Direct Corpus Interaction (DCI) removes embedding model, vector index, and top-k retrieval entirely; adopted by Anthropic (Claude Code), Windsurf, Cline, Devin, Sourcegraph Amp
- [AI Work Receipt](entries/ai-work-receipt.md) — Concept for an agent skill that generates structured AI work receipts — two arms: time-saved estimates (human baseline vs AI-assisted time) and artifact metrics (lines of code, functions/modules shipped, bugs logged/closed); aimed at giving managers and stakeholders visibility into AI productivity gains
- [Architect Loop](entries/architect-loop.md) — Research-backed cross-vendor agent loop with strong separation of concerns; excellent design patterns for gated, worktree-isolated multi-agent builds
- [Automation Credit Dependency Pattern](entries/automation-credit-dependency.md) — Map who gets credit for the work before automating it — treat visibility as a technical dependency; invisible automation can damage careers even when it works perfectly
- [Blockout Technique for Pixel Art Animation (Color-Coded Body-Part Blobs)](entries/blockout-pixel-art-animation.md) — Game-dev animation technique for pixel art sprites. Animating detailed pixel art frame by frame from the start is slow — each pose requires precise pixel placement, and fixing a bad pose means redoing detail work. A blockout instead assigns each body part (arm, leg, head, torso, weapon) a distinct flat color with no internal detail, producing a rough colored-blob version of the sprite. The full animation is blocked out first using only these color blobs, which is faster to pose and easier to revise since there is no detail to preserve; the blockout can be previewed and tested in-engine to validate timing and motion before any detail work begins. Once the blocked-out motion reads correctly, each pose is detailed individually — adding detail on top of an established pose is reported as easier than freehanding a detailed pose from scratch. Framed as an extension of the general game-dev 'blockout' practice (used for roughing in level geometry/environment art before detailing) applied to sprite animation, and overlaps with the traditional animation concepts of blocking/pose-to-pose workflows and silhouette-based pose readability checks.
- [Boris Cherny's CLAUDE.md](entries/boris-cherny-claude-md.md) — A widely-shared CLAUDE.md (attributed to Boris Cherny) codifying a Claude Code operating discipline: plan-mode-by-default, liberal subagent use, a self-improvement lessons loop, verification-before-done, demanding elegance, and autonomous bug fixing, plus a tasks/todo.md + tasks/lessons.md task-management convention.
- [Building Evals (Anthropic Cookbook)](entries/building-evals-anthropic.md) — Walks through the structure of offline LLM evaluations (prompt, output, golden answer, score) and three grading approaches — code-based, human, and model-based grading — along with design heuristics for building reliable eval sets
- [Chip-Damage Health Bars and Color-Coded Floating Damage Numbers (Hit-Feedback Game-Feel Techniques)](entries/health-bar-chip-damage-feedback.md) — Game-dev UI/feedback techniques for making hit-point damage feel satisfying without changing the underlying numbers. Health bar — on damage, a pale/orange "chip" trail is left behind at the bar's prior fill level and holds briefly before draining down to the new value (rather than snapping instantly), so the player sees how much was lost; the bar also shakes, flashes, and punches on each hit; its color shifts green→yellow→red as a threshold-based danger signal; healing fills back in smoothly with a sparkle effect at the fill edge. Floating damage numbers — numbers pop in, float upward, and fade out over roughly half a second to just over a second; rapid consecutive hits stack and arc to alternating sides so overlapping numbers stay legible; number size scales with hit magnitude; numbers are color-coded by type (e.g. white/normal, orange/critical, green/heal); critical hits render bold and with extra shake. Presented as a reusable feedback layer independent of the underlying damage math — the same damage value reads as more or less impactful purely from how it is animated and rendered.
- [Chunkless RAG (IBM)](entries/chunkless-rag.md) — RAG architecture that preserves document tree structure instead of splitting into fixed-size chunks — AI agents navigate the document hierarchy (table of contents, headings, sections) like a human reader, reading short section summaries to locate relevant content, then drilling into specific sections; requires structural document parsing (e.g. Docling) to reconstruct logical hierarchy from PDFs; trades higher latency and compute for better cross-section reasoning, context preservation, and reduced hallucination on complex documents
- [Claude Agent Teams](entries/agent-teams.md) — primary interactive dispatch path; subscription billing, no API credits needed
- [Claude Code as a Guardrailed Engineering Team (Solo-Operator Case Study)](entries/engineering-team-claude-code-setup.md) — Detailed solo-operator case study (45-year developer, $100/mo plan, 1,160 issues closed across 15 projects in 60 days) structuring Claude Code as a small team with enforced process rather than a chat window: rules live in PreToolUse hooks (not CLAUDE.md prose) once a correction repeats twice; a self-hosted GitLab plus a ~90-tool custom MCP server acts as the system of record with an issue state machine the model cannot bypass (raw API calls forbidden); work is routed across model tiers (Haiku/Sonnet/Opus, plus a non-generative classifier below Haiku) by task difficulty, with the main thread only planning/dispatching; memory is a typed, dated, version-controlled knowledge base instead of free text; and 'verify before asking or asserting' is a hard rule enforced through checks (file hashes, compile checks, render-then-view for documents) rather than trust.
- [Claude Code Fable Cost Playbook](entries/claude-code-fable-cost-playbook.md) — Six-technique playbook for reducing Claude Fable 5 spend inside Claude Code CLI — effort-level tuning, subagent model tiering, prompt-cache preservation, hard budget caps, and the advisor alternative pattern
- [Claude Code Local Model Switching](entries/claude-code-local-model-switching.md) — Shell alias technique for swapping Claude Code between cloud Anthropic, local Ollama/LM Studio, and API-based OSS models (OpenRouter/DeepSeek) via ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL and ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN environment variables; includes KV cache fix (CLAUDE_CODE_ATTRIBUTION_HEADER=0) and Qwen 3.6 35B config
- [Claude Native Subagents](entries/native-subagents.md) — lightweight HO consultation route within existing sessions; no separate billing
- [Cloud Session Context Repository Pattern](entries/cloud-session-context-repo.md) — Pattern for persistent multi-session Claude Code cloud workflows — shared context repo (CLAUDE.md + .gitmodules + session handoff notes + skills), launcher webapp generating parameterized session URLs, status feed for monitoring; git as the sync layer, no backend
- [Code Mode over MCP (execute_code pattern)](entries/code-mode-over-mcp.md) — Architecture pattern where an agent exposes a single execute_code tool instead of N MCP tools — the agent writes code against an SDK/API to perform actions, keeping token cost constant per turn instead of scaling linearly with tool count; 78–99% token savings at scale (benchmarked at 500+ tools); Cloudflare, Anthropic, and Bifrost implementations
- [CodeAct](entries/code-act.md) — CodeAct proposes executable Python code as a unified action space for LLM agents, replacing constrained JSON/text action formats, and includes the CodeActInstruct dataset, fine-tuned CodeActAgent models, and the M3ToolEval benchmark
- [Codex Workflow (viettran-edgeAI)](entries/codex-workflow.md) — Multi-agent orchestration architecture for Codex/Claude Code optimizing token usage within plan limits — 7 specialized roles (Main Agent, Companion/Luna, Investigator, Default Executor/Luna max, Senior Executor/Sol, Tester, Archivist) with fine-tuned batching, knowledge distribution via task-completion guides, and built-in per-agent token reporting; Sol xhigh ran 4h22m at 90% of 5h limit vs Astra at 260%; MIT
- [Coding-Agent TV Debloat over ADB](entries/coding-agent-adb-tv-debloat.md) — Pattern for using a coding agent (Claude Code in the source) to debloat an Android TV / Google TV over network ADB without root — enable developer options and debugging, give the agent the TV model and IP, have it back up the package list and write a restore script before changing anything, classify installed packages (tracking, ads, background extras, used apps) for the user to choose from, disable with pm disable-user rather than uninstall, and verify each change by sending remote key events and pulling screenshots; extras: lower animation scales, swap the stock launcher for a lightweight one; documented risks: bricking from disabling the wrong system package, loss of disabled features, small gains on 1 GB TVs, leave debugging on
- [Cold-Cache Session Resume Strategies (trim vs compact vs keep-warm)](entries/cold-cache-session-resume.md) — Practitioner techniques for resuming a long agent session after its prompt cache has expired (>5 min or >1 h idle) — the first resumed turn re-reads the whole prefix at full input price (author measured ~5x a warm turn); below ~150K context just continue, above it either trim (replace old tool results with one-line stubs, strip thinking blocks, no model call; same-provider only) or compact with a cheap model (e.g. Haiku summary + last 2 exchanges; works cross-provider); prevent coldness with UX warnings, keep-warm pings, or the API's 1-hour cache TTL (2x write vs 1.25x for 5-minute); never splice summaries into the middle of a warm session since the cache is prefix-based
- [Conductor: RL-Trained LLM Orchestrator](entries/conductor-orchestration.md) — Sakana AI paper (ICLR 2026) introducing a 7B 'Conductor' model trained with RL to orchestrate pools of worker LLMs — learns communication topologies, per-agent prompt engineering, and recursive self-delegation; achieves SOTA on LiveCodeBench and GPQA by coordinating frontier models.
- [Confidence-Probing Prompts](entries/confidence-probing-prompts.md) — Collection of meta-prompting patterns that surface agent blind spots — 'what are you least confident about' (forces enumeration of uninvestigated areas), 'what's the biggest thing I'm missing' (Altman), plus future-proofing, hidden-bias, and session-improvement variants
- [Contextual Retrieval (Anthropic Cookbook)](entries/contextual-retrieval-anthropic.md) — Demonstrates three progressive techniques for improving RAG retrieval quality — contextual embeddings (situating each chunk within its source document before embedding), contextual BM25 hybrid search, and reranking — achieving up to 95.26% Pass@10
- [Cutting Anticipation Frames for Input Responsiveness (Diablo 3 Barbarian Swing, Mario Jump)](entries/cut-anticipation-frames-responsiveness.md) — Game-dev technique for making player-controlled actions feel responsive by removing, rather than speeding up, the anticipation phase of an animation. Traditional animation splits an action into three roughly equal parts — anticipation (wind-up), action, and follow-through — but the anticipation portion delays the visible response to player input. Cites classic platformers (e.g. Super Mario), where the character begins moving upward immediately on jump input instead of crouching first, and Diablo 3's early development, where a barbarian's two-handed weapon swing originally reached back from a neutral pose before striking; speeding up that wind-up still read as sluggish and lost its sense of weight, while cutting the roughly 5–6 frames (about 180ms) of anticipation entirely — so the character instantly snaps to the fully wound-up pose the instant the player clicks — fixed the responsiveness complaints. Frames the fix in terms of silhouette — an instant, dramatic change in the character's on-screen shape gives the player an immediate visual read that the input registered, substituting for the anticipation frames as the feedback signal.
- [Decomposition Over Context: Chat Is the Wrong Abstraction](entries/decomposition-over-context.md) — Argues larger context windows aren't the fix for agentic-coding limits — task decomposition with explicit ownership, dependencies, and file boundaries is; illustrated with a TESTER/FIXER agent split and an issue-based parallel-implementation workflow
- [Directing and Verifying AI Coding Agents (What Separates Strong Engineers)](entries/directing-and-verifying-coding-agents.md) — Findings from a founder's informal benchmark scoring ~60 engineers (0-1000) on how well they direct, review, and verify a coding agent on a real task with deliberately planted half-failures: the dominant skill gap was specification quality and review behavior, not prompting; top scorers verify mechanism (which files changed, whether test files changed) rather than output alone, since output-only review never catches an agent weakening a test to go green; checking between turns beats one review at the end; a task costing 3x its neighbors signals a badly cut task, not inherent difficulty; and detecting a half-failure without an owner to act on it is worthless.
- [Disney's Animation Principles Applied to Game VFX (Anticipation, Squash & Stretch, Follow Through, Timing, Arcs)](entries/disney-animation-principles-game-vfx.md) — Game-dev technique for making a combat VFX effect (demonstrated on a thrown fireball) read as satisfying by applying five of Disney's twelve classical animation principles instead of adding more particles or better shaders — anticipation (the hero leans back while the fire gathers, then throws), squash and stretch (the fireball stretches in flight, the enemy squashes on hit), follow through (the hero's body settles after the throw, the trail lags behind, the burst flies on), timing (freeze the game for a few frames on the hit, i.e. hit-stop), and arcs (the fireball, sparks, and damage numbers travel on curved rather than straight paths). Framed as the same underlying asset read as flat or alive purely based on which of these principles are layered on top, independent of particle count or shader quality.
- [Docker-Isolated Agent Sandbox Pattern](entries/docker-isolated-agent-sandbox.md) — Architecture pattern for running multiple AI coding agents in Docker containers with filesystem isolation — agents access only explicitly shared files and connections, work in git worktrees, all changes visible and reviewable in git; includes port collision prevention, resource exhaustion guards, zombie process cleanup, and merge conflict prevention; reported 38% token savings vs uncontained agents
- [Dynamic Context Injection in Claude Code Skills](entries/dynamic-context-injection.md) — Claude Code SKILL.md feature — `!`<command>`` (inline) or fenced ` ```! ` blocks (multi-line) run shell commands before the skill body reaches Claude, splicing stdout into the prompt; enables deterministic, non-LLM routing of which instructions get loaded instead of prose like 'if X, read file Y'
- [Embodied Agent Design Patterns (Physical Robot)](entries/embodied-agent-design-patterns.md) — Four lessons from embedding an agent with persistent memory and tool access into a physical robot: reflexes must bypass the model, memory needs periodic consolidation and audit trails, tool access needs active restriction, and inline action-timing beats raw model capability for perceived intelligence
- [End-of-Session Review Questions](entries/end-of-session-review-questions.md) — Two standing end-of-session prompts ('what are you least confident about' and 'what am I probably missing') used to surface glossed-over assumptions and context gaps, with community variants and known failure modes
- [Fable as Orchestrator — Role-per-Model Split with Ticketed Review Loop](entries/fable-orchestrator-model-split.md) — Practitioner workflow for stretching Claude Fable usage limits by making Fable the orchestrator, not the worker — Haiku scouts locations, Sonnet researches and builds from specs, Opus refutes/reviews and debugs; extended with a ticket loop where an ephemeral Opus hand implements in a worktree, a cold Fable reviewer issues ACCEPT/REWORK verdicts, green-suite merges auto-revert on red, and the lead cuts releases
- [Fable Orchestrator Pattern (Multi-Model Delegation)](entries/fable-orchestrator-pattern.md) — Workflow pattern using Claude Fable as principal orchestrator with cheaper models (Sonnet, Opus, Codex/GPT-5.6) as execution workers — Fable plans and judges, workers implement and review in loops; achieves ~92–96% of Fable-solo quality at 40–63% of the cost; implemented via Claude Code agents, Codex CLI persistent threads, or the advisor tool
- [Five Levels of Chunking Strategies in RAG (Greg Kamradt's taxonomy)](entries/five-levels-of-chunking.md) — Taxonomy of RAG text-splitting strategies, from Greg Kamradt's '5 Levels of Text Splitting' (these are notes on that video): Level 1 fixed-size (character/sentence splitters), Level 2 recursive (hierarchical separators), Level 3 document/structure-based (respect headings, tables, code), Level 4 semantic (split at embedding-similarity breakpoints), Level 5 agentic (an LLM decides chunk boundaries from propositions). Higher levels trade cost/complexity for chunks that better match meaning; match the strategy to the data.
- [Five-Beat Boss Entrance Sequence (Lock, Wait, Reveal, Name, Sell-the-Size)](entries/boss-fight-entrance-design.md) — Game-dev technique for staging a boss encounter's opening moments as a five-beat sequence rather than a plain spawn-and-health-bar pop-in — lock the arena (seal the path the player entered by, e.g. collapsing it, removing retreat as an option), make the player wait (empty arena, low rumble, dust settling, building tension before the boss appears), reveal the boss slowly by panning the camera from its feet up to its face rather than showing it all at once, slam its name on screen in large text, fill a large health bar from empty rather than showing it already full (communicating scale), then sell its size with a low camera angle, screen-shaking footsteps, and a roar that triggers screen shake, a dust blast, and arena-wide reaction. Framed as reusable staging independent of the specific boss's stats or moveset — the same boss model reads as a non-event or as a threat purely based on how the entrance is directed.
- [Five-Effect Card Feel Technique (Hover, Pick Up, Sway, Snap, Sound)](entries/card-feel-five-effects.md) — Game-dev technique for making a playable card feel satisfying instead of flat, via five layered effects applied on top of an otherwise unchanged card-in-hand interaction — (1) hover, where sliding across the hand pops each card up and leans it toward the cursor, though grabbing one barely lifts it off the table; (2) pick up, where lifting a card high spreads its shadow outward beneath it, though the card stays visually flat while dragged; (3) sway, where the card leans in the direction it's dragged and swings back when released, though it originally teleported into place on drop; (4) snap, where the card flies into its target slot, overshoots, and settles, with the rest of the hand sliding over to close the gap, though this step is silent; (5) sound, adding a soft flick noise on grab and a slap noise on landing. Framed as a reusable five-layer pipeline — the same baseline card-in-hand interaction (described as "about as fun as filling in a form") reads as a satisfying "big play" purely from how many of these layers are stacked on top of it, independent of the card game's actual rules or content.
- [Five-Step Dialogue Presentation Technique (Typewriter, Pauses, Sound, Text Effects, Speaker Animation)](entries/dialogue-feel-five-steps.md) — Game-dev technique for making NPC dialogue feel alive instead of appearing as a flat wall of text, via five layered presentation steps — (1) typewriter effect reveals letters one at a time so the player reads along at the character's pace; (2) pauses hold a short beat on each comma and period, giving the line timing instead of a flat reading speed; (3) a tiny sound blip plays on every revealed letter, pitched to suit the speaking character; (4) text effects apply per-word motion keyed to tone, e.g. happy words wave and angry words shake; (5) the speaker's portrait or model bobs while talking and its facial expression switches between states (e.g. happy to furious) tied to the line's content. Framed as a reusable layered presentation pipeline independent of the actual dialogue content — the same line of text reads as flat or as a performed character purely from how these five steps are applied on top of it.
- [Flattened Secondary Mesh as a Stylized Fake Shadow (Instead of Dynamic Shadow Rendering)](entries/flattened-mesh-fake-shadows.md) — Game-dev shadow technique that replaces an engine's built-in dynamic shadow system with a hand-controlled stand-in: a second, simplified version of the character mesh is flattened onto the floor via a material, and that flattened silhouette is used as the character's shadow. Reported benefits are twofold — it saves the performance cost of real-time shadow/lighting calculations, and it gives the artist direct control over the shadow's shape and softness so it can be made 'punchy' and stylized to match the game's art style rather than looking like a physically-accurate soft/dynamic shadow. Demonstrated in Unreal Engine by the two-person dev team of the upcoming roguelike 'Barrow and Blade' as one of three non-standard optimization techniques.
- [Functional Differentiation of Status Effects (Fire/Poison/Bleed by Role, Not Magnitude)](entries/status-effect-functional-differentiation.md) — Game-dev design technique for avoiding redundant damage-over-time status effects — when fire, poison, and bleed differ only in how much damage they deal, players converge on the single strongest one and the rest go unused; the fix is giving each effect a distinct mechanical role instead of a different number. Demonstrated with three effects: fire spreads on kill (igniting the three nearest enemies, which can chain further), poison scales (every 10 stacks adds 10% increased damage taken from all sources), and bleed bursts (at 5 stacks, it detonates all remaining stored damage at once) — giving fire a horde-clearing role, poison a sustained-fight role, and bleed a fast single-target-burst role.
- [Governance-First Multi-Agent Architecture](entries/governance-first-multi-agent-architecture.md) — An architecture pattern for solo-use multi-agent systems built governance-first, using a four-tier hierarchy (lead agent, least-privilege specialists, an independent audit/watcher, and a human owner) with structural approval gates on irreversible actions
- [Graph Engineering (Agentic Pattern)](entries/graph-engineering.md) — Emerging paradigm for designing multi-agent AI systems as explicit directed graphs — deterministic backbone orchestrating LLM-powered nodes, typed edges for control flow/data passing, declarative topology as a versionable artifact; supersedes single-agent loop patterns at scale
- [GVS5H — Ledger-Based Zero-Shot Self-Orchestration](entries/gvs5h-ledger-orchestration.md) — Training-free multi-agent coding method where fresh instances of one model decompose problems and coordinate through a shared filesystem (plan, notes, current solution); Qwen3.8-27B rises from 69.2% to 92.4% pass@1 on LiveCodeBench Hard (exceeding Fable 5's 90.4%); GPT-5.6-Terra reaches 88.0% at 19% of Fable 5's cost; gains attributed to decomposition and persistent context; MIT/CC-BY-4.0
- [High-Impact CLAUDE.md Lines (r/ClaudeCode Crowd-Sourced)](entries/claude-md-high-impact-lines.md) — Crowd-sourced thread of specific CLAUDE.md instruction lines practitioners report changed agent behavior (not style preferences) — read-all-then-edit-once batching, don't-break-the-queue-for-corrections, attribute-mistakes-to-instruction-gaps-not-apologies, YAGNI scope discipline with explicit escalation, cheap-model subagent self-review, don't-edit-tests-to-pass (paired with a hook since the instruction alone got the tests deleted instead), and fact-check-by-reopening-the-file-before-claiming-what-code-does.
- [Instagram Saved-Reels-to-Obsidian Knowledge Graph Pipeline](entries/instagram-saved-reels-obsidian-knowledge-graph.md) — A personal-archiving technique that uses yt-dlp to bulk-download a user's own saved Instagram reels (about 500 in the creator's case), then an AI-assisted script transcribes each reel and extracts named entities and proper nouns, categorizes it (memes, tutorials, conspiracies, health, etc.), and writes a node into an Obsidian vault with an executive summary, tags, the source video's hash, key takeaways, and screenshots; Obsidian's graph view then shows reel-to-reel links, and the creator deletes each reel from Instagram's saved library after scraping it and manually prunes the resulting graph.
- [Knowledge Graph Construction (Anthropic Cookbook)](entries/knowledge-graph-construction-anthropic.md) — An end-to-end pipeline for constructing knowledge graphs from unstructured documents using Claude prompts instead of trained NER/RE models, covering structured-output entity/triple extraction, LLM-based entity resolution, NetworkX graph assembly, hub-node summarization, and multi-hop querying with citations
- [Knowledge in the Folder, Behavior in the Model: A Fine-Tuning vs. RAG Split](entries/knowledge-folder-behavior-model-split.md) — A three-step strategy for deciding what to fine-tune versus what to retrieve: keep facts (pricing, policies, specs) in documents the model reads at answer time rather than baking them into weights, since fine-tuning changes model behavior, not factual recall, so tuning on company documents still produces factual errors; tune only for behavior (output format, tone, how to answer a complaint) using a few hundred clean, human-approved examples of one task rather than thousands of unreviewed ones; and do that tuning on a small open-weight model run on a single workstation, A/B tested against the untuned baseline before deployment.
- [LLM Council](entries/llm-council.md) — Andrej Karpathy's local web app (ChatGPT-like UI) that sends one query to multiple LLMs via OpenRouter, has each model anonymously rank the others' responses on accuracy/insight, then has a designated 'Chairman' model compile a final synthesized answer; three-stage pipeline (first opinions → anonymized cross-review/ranking → Chairman synthesis). FastAPI/Python backend, React/Vite frontend, uv + npm tooling. Explicitly described by the author as '99% vibe coded' as a weekend hack, unsupported and not intended to be improved further.
- [Local Model + Cloud Adversarial Workflow](entries/local-model-adversarial-workflow.md) — Multi-model orchestration pattern — local 27B model (Qwen) handles drafting, planning, and implementation; cloud models (Opus, GPT) do adversarial code reviews; repeated review loops until green; domain-specific MCP tools for database/web verification reduce hallucinations and token costs
- [Local Model Harness Patterns](entries/local-model-harness-patterns.md) — Harness design patterns for running local LLMs as coding agents — subagent delegation, reasoning-level routing, loop detection with context cleanup, and speculative decoding
- [Meta^n](entries/meta-n.md) — Research prototype (MinnesotaNLP, arXiv 2608.24735 / 2509.24175) for recursive self-improvement of agent process, not just answers: a single fixed meta-operation Ω reads execution traces from the layer(s) below and writes executable code (a pre_process hook plus a code_library) injected into that layer, applied repeatedly to build a stack of emergent meta-layers (roles arise from context, not design) driven by an archive-based evolutionary search (EvolutionaryOrchestrator). Supports 9 benchmarks (CO-Bench, Symptom2Disease, LawBench charge prediction, TerminalBench 2.0, SWE-bench Verified, AlphaEvolve Math, Symbolic Regression, AlgoTune, ARC-AGI-2). Python ≥3.11, MIT; author describes it as an exploratory research prototype, not headline results.
- [Minimal Agent Integration Philosophy](entries/minimal-agent-integration.md) — Practitioner philosophy arguing that production AI agents succeed with one deeply-connected surface (typically inbox+calendar via MCP) rather than broad multi-tool architectures; narrow-and-deep beats wide-and-shallow for auditability and sustained use
- [MISTAKES.md — a self-accumulating agent error log](entries/mistakes-md-log.md) — Lightweight Claude Code technique: keep a MISTAKES.md in the repo plus one CLAUDE.md line ('Log mistakes in MISTAKES.md (what happened, root cause, prevention).'). Whenever the agent breaks something or is corrected, it appends an entry (what happened / root cause / consequence / the rule that prevents a repeat), newest first — no tooling, plugin, or vector store. Two reported effects: the agent starts citing and avoiding past mistakes, and a failure that recurs 4–5 times graduates from a logged mistake into a hard rule in CLAUDE.md. MISTAKES.md accumulates evidence; CLAUDE.md enforces it.
- [Nine Slicing (9-Slice) for Scalable UI Panels](entries/nine-slicing-ui-panels.md) — Game-dev UI technique for resizing a panel/box sprite to any dimension without distorting its border art. Naively scaling a textured panel along an axis stretches the corners and looks worse the more it's scaled.
